❯  I want to create a standalone tool that helps me visualize how a viral       
  infection spreads and map out some of the key parameters, like the SIR        
  diagram, to understand what impact different variables have on the spread of  
  the disease, peak infection rate, and the number of people infected. To be    
  clear, the SIR diagram stands for susceptible, infected, and removed people.  
  The goal of this exercise is to minimize the peak infection rate because it   
  has an impact on the medical                                                  
    infrastructure of a given city, country, etc., and also to minimize the     
  number of people who are removed or recovered from the disease, because a     
  certain percentage of those people will not survive. The way in which we will 
  build it is that I want to have a standalone tool that helps me visualize     
  the spread of infection. There are multiple variables that we should be able  
  to control, rerun the simulation, and understand the impact that it has on    
  the SIR diagram. To start with, I                                             
    want to have a way to visualize people who are infected. Think of a square  
  that represents a city, country, or geographical entity. There are dots that  
  represent humans wandering around randomly within that square. Each dot has a 
  radius associated with it, which is the infection radius. Anyone who comes    
  closer to the person and is within that radius is susceptible to getting      
  infected if that person was infected with the disease. I want to have a way   
  to visualize this, and the dots                                               
    should be moving around in some kind of pattern. I should have a global     
  knob that lets me control the speed at which these dots travel. I want to     
  have a way to speed up the time, and every other parameter we discussed       
  should use that as a multiplier. For example, if we discuss how long a person 
  should be in contact with an infected person to catch the disease, that       
  parameter should be scaled based on the value of this global control. The     
  global control gives us a way to multiply all                                 
    the parameters that are temporal. I also want to visualize the radius of    
  infection for every person. Think about how we can animate a concentric       
  circle around each person that represents the infection radius. You can go    
  through the attached PDF to understand how I want to lay it out. I want to    
  make sure this is built as a standalone web application, so I don't need any  
  other dependencies. We should try to minimize dependencies while ensuring we  
  have a very polished version of the                                           
    tool that has been implemented. I should be able to run this in a web       
  browser and that's the only dependency I want. Please make sure to include    
  relevant CSS and frameworks to run this. The visualization should be a time   
  series showing how many people are susceptible, how many people are infected, 
  and how many people have recovered. Susceptible people should be represented  
  with a green dot, infected people with a red dot, and recovered people with   
  a blue dot. Refer to the attached                                             
    PDF to understand how the layout of this tool should be in the web browser. 
  There are some other parameters that I want to tune, and they should show up  
  under the arm parameters section. These parameters are categorized under      
  different subheadings: - Things which are in personal control (for example, a 
  social distancing factor). Social distancing factor is represented as a       
  percentage of the total population that follows social distancing. Social     
  distancing would mean that people do                                          
    not come closer than the infection radius of that disease. When the dots    
  are moving, X% of the people (which is attunable) should stay away from any   
  other person and be at the very minimum as far away as the infection radius   
  for that disease. Let's put together a plan for implementing this. Ask me any 
  clarifying questions that you may have.

⏺ This changes the picture significantly — and now the phrase "arm parameters"
  from your original message makes sense. The PDF shows a two-arm A/B comparison
  layout, like a clinical trial:

  - Top (full width): Global controls + title (Play/Pause, Reset, global
  speed/time multiplier).
  - Control arm (row 1): its own parameters · its own simulation · its own SIR
  chart.
  - Test arm (row 2): its own parameters · its own simulation · its own SIR
  chart.

  So the tool runs two simulations side-by-side, driven by the same global
  clock, letting you change a parameter in the test arm (e.g., social-distancing
  %) and directly see its impact on peak infection and total removed versus the
  control. That's a great design for your stated goal.

  One design decision this raises that genuinely affects the build:

⏺ User answered Claude's questions:
  ⎿  · For a fair A/B comparison, should both arms start from the same 
     randomized initial state (same dot positions, velocities, and 
     initially-infected people), so any difference in outcome is caused only by 
     the parameters you changed? → #1 and any changes to any parameter should be
     highlighted in a different color so that we can look at what's different 
     between the two setups. This is also a broader requirement that I should be
     able to run experiments by changing one of these parameters and be able to
     see the differences that this change brings about in the overall SIR 
     diagrams.
     · By default, how should the two arms' parameters relate when the tool 
     loads? →  by default, there should only be one view, which lets me 
     visualize the treatment, but there should be a toggle that helps me toggle 
     between the single view and the test and control view so that I can run 
     those experiments

⏺ That completes the picture. Key refinements captured:

  - Default = single view (the treatment). A toggle switches to the two-arm
  Control + Test comparison view from the PDF.
  - Shared seed (default ON, toggleable) so both arms start identical and only
  parameter changes drive divergence.
  - Changed parameters are highlighted in a distinct color in compare mode, so
  you can see at a glance what differs between arms — enabling clean
  one-variable experiments on the SIR outcomes.

  I now have everything I need. Let me write the plan.

⏺ Updated plan
  ⎿  /plan to preview

⏺ The plan is written. Here's a summary before you approve:

  What we're building: a standalone, browser-only agent-based SIR simulator
  structured as an A/B experiment tool (matching your PDF) — a Control arm and
  Test arm side-by-side under one global clock.

  Key design points locked in:
  - Separate index.html / styles.css / plain .js files — opens by double-click,
  no build step.
  - Chart.js vendored locally so it runs fully offline.
  - Single view by default (the treatment); a toggle reveals the two-arm Compare
  view.
  - Shared seed (default ON, toggleable) → arms start identical so only
  parameter changes drive divergence.
  - Changed test-arm parameters highlighted in a distinct color with the delta —
  your one-variable experiment loop.
  - Parameter groups: Disease · Personal control · Public policy · Simulation &
  environment, driven by a params.js schema so the extra parameters we deferred
  (infection probability, mortality, more policy levers) are each a one-line add
  later.
  - Two global temporal knobs: dot speed (spatial) and time multiplier
  (accelerates the sim clock, auto-scaling all temporal params).

  Let me load the exit-plan tool and present this for approval.

     │ infected person's radius long enough become infected. State is          │

     │ so the user                                                             │
     │ can change a single parameter in the test arm and directly compare its  │
     │ impact on the SIR                                                       │
     │ curves. This directly serves the goal of finding interventions that     │
     │ lower peak infection                                                    │
     │ and total removed.                                                      │

     │                                                                         │
     │ Greenfield project in /Users/sakshamg/workspace/sims/. No existing code │
     │ to reuse.                                                               │
     │ Plain ES5/ES6 classic scripts sharing a global App namespace (avoids    │
     │ file:// ES-module                                                       │
     │ CORS issues so the tool opens by double-click).                         │
     │                                                                         │
     │ Files                                                                   │
     │                                                                         │
     │ - index.html — page skeleton: global control bar, two arm rows (each =  │
     │ param panel +                                                           │
     │ <canvas> sim + <canvas> chart), loads lib/chart.umd.min.js then the app │
     │ scripts in                                                              │
     │ order.                                                                  │
     │ - styles.css — CSS-grid layout matching the PDF, theme, param-panel     │
     │ styling, the                                                            │
     │ diff-highlight color, S/I/R legend colors, responsive sizing.           │
     │ - lib/chart.umd.min.js — vendored Chart.js (downloaded during           │
     │ implementation).                                                        │
     │ - src/rng.js — seeded PRNG (mulberry32) so a seed → identical agent     │
     │ init across arms.                                                       │
     │ - src/params.js — single source of truth for parameters: array of       │
     │ {key, label, group, min, max, step, default, temporal} definitions.     │
     │ Drives both the UI                                                      │
     │ and the simulation. Adding a future parameter = one entry here. Groups: │
     │   - Disease: infectionRadius, contactDurationToInfect (sim-sec,         │
     │ temporal),                                                              │
     │ infectiousDuration (sim-sec, temporal).                                 │
     │   - Personal control: socialDistancingPct (% of population that keeps ≥ │
     │ infection                                                               │
     │ radius from others).                                                    │
     │   - Public policy: scaffolded group (starts minimal — e.g. a            │
     │ placeholder such as                                                     │
     │ quarantineOnInfectionPct, kept simple; populated in the later           │
     │ brainstorm).                                                            │
     │   - Simulation & environment: population, initialInfected.              │
     │ (Dot speed and time multiplier are global, not per-arm — see global     │
     │ controls.)                                                              │
     │ - src/agent.js — Agent (position, velocity, state S/I/R, infectedTimer, │
     │ per-neighbor exposureTime, isDistancer). Behaviors: bounded random-walk │
     │ wander;                                                                 │
     │ distancers apply a separation steer away from any agent within the      │
     │ infection radius.                                                       │
     │ - src/simulation.js — Simulation (one instance per arm): owns agents +  │
     │ its own RNG                                                             │
     │ seeded from the shared/independent seed; step(simDt) advances movement, │
     │ accumulates                                                             │
     │ contact time, converts S→I (contact ≥ contactDurationToInfect) and I→R  │
     │ (timer ≥                                                                │
     │ infectiousDuration); records SIR history (throttled, capped length).    │
     │ render(ctx)                                                             │
     │ draws rings (faint for all, animated pulse for infected) + color-coded  │
     │ dots + live                                                             │
     │ S/I/R counts. Deterministic agent generation order so changing          │
     │ population still keeps                                                  │
     │ the leading agents identical across arms.                               │
     │ - src/charts.js — builds one Chart.js line/stacked-area chart per arm;  │
     │ streams SIR                                                             │
     │ history with update throttling.                                         │
     │ - src/ui.js — builds param panels from params.js; wires sliders/number  │
     │ inputs;                                                                 │
     │ computes and applies diff highlighting (test vs control) in compare     │
     │ mode; handles the                                                       │
     │ single⇄compare toggle and global controls.                              │
     │ - src/app.js — bootstrap: creates control + test Simulations, wires     │
     │ global controls,                                                        │
     │ runs the single requestAnimationFrame loop that drives both arms off    │
     │ one clock,                                                              │
     │ and calls render + chart update.                                        │
     │                                                                         │
     │ Time model (satisfies the two global knobs)                             │
     │                                                                         │
     │ - Time multiplier accelerates the sim clock: simDt = realDt *           │
     │ timeMultiplier.                                                         │
     │ Because contactDurationToInfect and infectiousDuration are defined in   │
     │ sim-seconds,                                                            │
     │ they automatically scale with the multiplier — matching "multiply all   │
     │ temporal params."                                                       │
     │ - Dot speed is a separate spatial knob multiplying each agent's wander  │
     │ velocity.                                                               │
     │ - Both knobs are global and apply to both arms simultaneously.          │
     │                                                                         │
     │ Infection & distancing logic                                            │
     │                                                                         │
     │ - A susceptible agent accumulates contact time while within             │
     │ infectionRadius of any                                                  │
     │ infected agent; crossing contactDurationToInfect → infected.            │
     │ (Probability-per-contact                                                │
     │ deferred.)                                                              │
     │ - Infected → Removed once infectedTimer ≥ infectiousDuration.           │
     │ - socialDistancingPct of agents are flagged distancers who steer to     │
     │ keep ≥                                                                  │
     │ infectionRadius separation; the rest wander freely.                     │
     │                                                                         │
     │ Shared-seed comparison                                                  │
     │                                                                         │
     │ - When ON, both arms seed their RNG identically → identical positions,  │
     │ velocities, and                                                         │
     │ initially-infected set (when sim/env params match). Toggle OFF →        │
     │ independent randomness.                                                 │
     │                                                                         │

⏺ Yes — confirming, and I'll make it explicit in the plan. The global time 
  multiplier scales the entire simulation clock, so every temporal quantity is
  affected by it, including dot movement:

  The loop computes simDt = realDt × timeMultiplier, and everything integrates
  against simDt:
  - Dot movement: position += velocity × dotSpeed × simDt → dots travel faster
  when you speed up time.
  - Contact time to infect: accumulates in simDt → the infection threshold is
  reached proportionally faster.
  - Infectious/recovery duration: the infected timer advances in simDt →
  recovery happens faster too.

  The separate dot-speed knob just sets the baseline wander speed; the time 
  multiplier compounds on top of it and equally affects contact time, recovery,
  and every other temporal parameter — so at, say, 3× everything (movement,
  transmission, recovery) unfolds 3× faster in real time, and the relative
  dynamics stay identical. Let me update the plan to state this explicitly.

⏺ Updated plan
  ⎿  /plan to preview

     edit
     Plan: Standalone Viral-Spread / SIR Simulator (A/B experiment tool)

     Context

     The user wants a standalone, browser-only tool to visualize how a viral
     infection
     spreads through a population and to understand how tunable parameters
     affect three
     outcomes they care about:

     1. Peak infection rate (strains medical infrastructure), and
     2. Total number removed/recovered (a fraction of whom die).

     The tool is an agent-based simulation: dots (people) wander randomly inside
     a square
     ("city"), each with a visible infection radius ring. Susceptible people
     within an
     infected person's radius long enough become infected. State is color-coded
     S = green, I = red, R = blue, and a SIR time-series chart tracks the
     counts.

     Crucially, per the attached layout PDF, this is an A/B experiment ("arm") 
     tool:
     a Control arm and a Test arm run side-by-side under one global clock, so
     the user
     can change a single parameter in the test arm and directly compare its
     impact on the SIR
     curves. This directly serves the goal of finding interventions that lower
     peak infection
     and total removed.

     Confirmed decisions

     - Packaging: separate index.html + styles.css + multiple plain .js files
     (classic <script> tags, no build step, opens via double-click / file://).
     - Charting: Chart.js — vendored locally (lib/chart.umd.min.js) so it also
     works
     fully offline (honors "only dependency is the browser"). CDN link kept as a
     comment.
     - Determinism: shared seed, default ON, toggleable. Both arms start from an
     identical seeded initial state; only parameter differences cause
     divergence.
     - Default layout: single view (one arm, the "treatment"). A toggle reveals
     the
     two-arm Control + Test comparison view.
     - Changed-parameter highlighting: in compare mode, any test-arm parameter
     differing
     from the control is highlighted in a distinct color with the delta shown.
     - Parameter groups (subheadings): Disease · Personal control · Public
     policy ·
     Simulation & environment.
     - Extra epidemiological parameters (infection probability, mortality, more
     policy
     levers) are intentionally deferred — v1 nails the framework and makes
     adding params
     trivial via a schema. Mortality/deaths is scaffolded but not the v1 focus.

     Layout (from Untitled presentation.pdf)

     ┌──────────────── Global Controls, title (full width) ───────────────┐
     │ ▶ Play  ⟲ Reset   Dot speed[===]  Time ×[===]  ☑Shared seed
     [Single│Compare]
     ├───────────────┬──────────────────────────┬─────────────────────────┤
     │ Control arm   │   Simulation (canvas)     │   SIR chart (Chart.js)  │  ←
     row hidden
     │ parameters    │   dots + rings, S/I/R     │   S/I/R over time       │
     in single view
     ├───────────────┼──────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────┤
     │ Test arm      │   Simulation (canvas)     │   SIR chart (Chart.js)  │  ←
     the arm shown
     │ parameters    │   dots + rings, S/I/R     │   S/I/R over time       │
     in single view
     └───────────────┴──────────────────────────┴─────────────────────────┘
     Implemented with CSS Grid. Single view renders only the treatment row;
     toggling to
     Compare reveals both rows.

     Architecture

     Greenfield project in /Users/sakshamg/workspace/sims/. No existing code to
     reuse.
     Plain ES5/ES6 classic scripts sharing a global App namespace (avoids
     file:// ES-module
     CORS issues so the tool opens by double-click).

     Files

     - index.html — page skeleton: global control bar, two arm rows (each =
     param panel +
     <canvas> sim + <canvas> chart), loads lib/chart.umd.min.js then the app
     scripts in
     order.
     - styles.css — CSS-grid layout matching the PDF, theme, param-panel
     styling, the
     diff-highlight color, S/I/R legend colors, responsive sizing.
     - lib/chart.umd.min.js — vendored Chart.js (downloaded during
     implementation).
     - src/rng.js — seeded PRNG (mulberry32) so a seed → identical agent init
     across arms.
     - src/params.js — single source of truth for parameters: array of
     {key, label, group, min, max, step, default, temporal} definitions. Drives
     both the UI
     and the simulation. Adding a future parameter = one entry here. Groups:
       - Disease: infectionRadius, contactDurationToInfect (sim-sec, temporal),
     infectiousDuration (sim-sec, temporal).
       - Personal control: socialDistancingPct (% of population that keeps ≥
     infection
     radius from others).
       - Public policy: scaffolded group (starts minimal — e.g. a placeholder
     such as
     quarantineOnInfectionPct, kept simple; populated in the later brainstorm).
       - Simulation & environment: population, initialInfected.
     (Dot speed and time multiplier are global, not per-arm — see global
     controls.)
     - src/agent.js — Agent (position, velocity, state S/I/R, infectedTimer,
     per-neighbor exposureTime, isDistancer). Behaviors: bounded random-walk
     wander;
     distancers apply a separation steer away from any agent within the
     infection radius.
     - src/simulation.js — Simulation (one instance per arm): owns agents + its
     own RNG
     seeded from the shared/independent seed; step(simDt) advances movement,
     accumulates
     contact time, converts S→I (contact ≥ contactDurationToInfect) and I→R
     (timer ≥
     infectiousDuration); records SIR history (throttled, capped length).
     render(ctx)
     draws rings (faint for all, animated pulse for infected) + color-coded dots
     + live
     S/I/R counts. Deterministic agent generation order so changing population
     still keeps
     the leading agents identical across arms.
     - src/charts.js — builds one Chart.js line/stacked-area chart per arm;
     streams SIR
     history with update throttling.
     - src/ui.js — builds param panels from params.js; wires sliders/number
     inputs;
     computes and applies diff highlighting (test vs control) in compare mode;
     handles the
     single⇄compare toggle and global controls.
     - src/app.js — bootstrap: creates control + test Simulations, wires global
     controls,
     runs the single requestAnimationFrame loop that drives both arms off one
     clock,
     and calls render + chart update.

     Time model (satisfies the two global knobs)

     - Time multiplier accelerates the entire sim clock: simDt = realDt * 
     timeMultiplier,
     and everything integrates against simDt, so every temporal quantity is
     influenced
     by the multiplier:
       - Dot movement: position += velocity * dotSpeed * simDt → dots travel
     faster when
     time is sped up.
       - Contact time to infect: accumulates in simDt → contactDurationToInfect
     threshold reached proportionally faster.
       - Infectious/recovery duration: infectedTimer advances in simDt →
     recovery
     happens faster.
     At e.g. 3× all of movement, transmission, and recovery unfold 3× faster in
     real time
     while relative dynamics stay identical.
     - Dot speed is a separate global knob that sets the baseline wander
     velocity; the
     time multiplier compounds on top of it (and on contact time, recovery,
     etc.).
     - Both knobs are global and apply to both arms simultaneously.

     Infection & distancing logic

     - A susceptible agent accumulates contact time while within infectionRadius
     of any
     infected agent; crossing contactDurationToInfect → infected.
     (Probability-per-contact
     deferred.)
     - Infected → Removed once infectedTimer ≥ infectiousDuration.
     - socialDistancingPct of agents are flagged distancers who steer to keep ≥
     infectionRadius separation; the rest wander freely.

     Shared-seed comparison

     - When ON, both arms seed their RNG identically → identical positions,
     velocities, and
     initially-infected set (when sim/env params match). Toggle OFF →
     independent randomness.

     Implementation steps

     1. Scaffold index.html + styles.css with the CSS-grid layout (global bar +
     two arm
     rows) and S/I/R legend; get the empty shell rendering correctly in the
     browser.
     2. Vendor Chart.js into lib/.
     3. rng.js, then params.js schema.
     4. agent.js + simulation.js: movement, rings, infection, S→I→R, SIR
     history. Verify a
     single sim animates and spreads.
     5. charts.js: per-arm SIR chart streaming.
     6. ui.js: build panels from schema, wire controls, single⇄compare toggle,
     diff
     highlighting.
     7. app.js: dual-arm loop off one clock; shared-seed toggle; dot-speed +
     time-multiplier;
     Play/Pause/Reset.
     8. Polish: colors, ring pulse animation, counts overlay, responsive layout,
     sensible
     defaults (e.g. population 200, initialInfected 3) and population cap for
     performance.

     Verification

     - Open index.html directly in a browser (double-click) and via
     python3 -m http.server in the project dir; confirm it works offline
     (Chart.js vendored).
     - Single view: dots wander inside the square; every dot shows an
     infection-radius ring;
     infected dots (red) infect nearby susceptibles (green) after sustained
     contact; infected
     turn removed (blue) after the infectious duration; SIR chart tracks counts;
     peak infection
     visible as the red curve's max.
     - Global controls: Play/Pause/Reset work; dot-speed slider changes travel
     speed;
     time-multiplier visibly accelerates spread and recovery for both arms;
     changes apply to
     both arms.
     - Compare toggle: reveals Control + Test rows; with shared seed ON and
     identical params,
     both sims are pixel-identical; changing one test-arm parameter (e.g. raise
     socialDistancingPct) makes the arms diverge and that parameter is 
     highlighted;
     the test arm's SIR chart shows a lower/flatter infection peak —
     demonstrating the core
     experiment loop.
     - Optional: use the browser MCP tools to launch the page and capture
     screenshots for a
     visual check during implementation.

     Out of scope (deferred, by design)

     - Additional parameters to brainstorm later: infection probability per
     contact, mortality
     rate + death counter, richer public-policy levers (quarantine, lockdown,
     testing). The
     params.js schema is built so each is a one-line addition.

❯ I want to change the visualization a little. The susceptible should start     
  from the number of people who are susceptible to the infection, but for       
  infection and removed rates, you should count the current number of people    
  who are infected and the current number of people who are removed. I want to  
  understand the peaks in the infection rates over time. This would help us     
  understand how much pressure this epidemic would have on the medical          
  infrastructure. It's important to understand the number of infections going   
  up and then coming down, and the recovered or removed population going up and 
  stabilizing at a certain point.                                               

⏺ Good refinement — and it makes the chart much more useful for your goal. Right
  now the three series are stacked (each band sits on top of the others), so
  the y-axis just climbs to the total population and you can't actually see the
  infected curve rise and fall. You want each line to show its true current 
  count:

  - Susceptible — starts at population − initialInfected and declines.
  - Infected — current infected: rises to a peak, then falls (the wave that
  stresses medical capacity).
  - Removed — current removed: rises and plateaus.

  The counts I record are already the true current counts, so the fix is to
  un-stack the chart. I'll also add a dashed peak-infection reference line (a
  tiny inline Chart.js plugin, no new dependency) since understanding that peak
  is the whole point. Let me update charts.js.

      23 +      if (peak <= 0) { return; }                               
      24 +      var yScale = chart.scales.y;

  index.html. When you play it now, you'll see the classic SIR shape: the red
  infected curve rising to its peak and coming back down, and the blue removed
  curve climbing and flattening out. Comparing arms, the flatter/lower the red

  trade-off you're optimizing.

❯ Can we add some kind of a circle around the dots to show the infection

  concentric circle so that it grows from the dot representing the person to    
  the full radius of infection. Do that randomly so I want to make sure that    
  the animation only starts when someone gets infected.                         

⏺ Good idea — that makes the "who is currently spreading" much clearer and ties
  the animation to the moment of infection. Here's exactly what I'll change:

  - Rings only for infected dots — susceptible (green) and removed (blue) dots
  lose their rings entirely.
  - Expanding ripple — each infected dot emits a concentric circle that grows
  from the dot out to the full infection radius, fading as it expands, then
  repeats.
  - Starts at infection, naturally staggered — I'll anchor each dot's animation
  to the moment it becomes infected (stamped on the first frame it's infected).
  Since people get infected at different times, the ripples are automatically
  out of phase ("random"), not a synchronized global pulse. I'll drive it off
  the real-time render clock so it stays smooth regardless of the time
  multiplier.

  Let me update agent.js and simulation.js.

  distancing), so you can, e.g., set Test hygiene to 80% and watch its curve

❯ I want to implement a global control called population mode. The first option 
  in that mode is the one that we have right now, where every single human      
  operates individually, but this is not how typical societies work. In typical 
  societies, people would live together in a group of anywhere between 1 and 4  
  people.                                                                       
  Once this mode has been selected, I want to have a global control through     
  which we can tune the maximum size of the household. Let's start with 4 for   
  now, but there should be a slider that lets people choose a different value.  
  Divide the existing population randomly between groups of 1, 2, 3, or 4       
  people. One is the case where people live individually, and there would be    
  some groups living in groups of 2, 3, or 4 people.                            
  Once this mode is also enabled, I want to enable a setting that then shows up 
  in the control and treatment group tunable parameters section, which          
  represents the amount of time people spend outside of this group. This is to  
  model a typical society where families would live together, but on a certain  
  probability basis, one or more people from that group would go out and meet   
  other people.                                                                 
  The way in which this should work is that once this mode has been selected,   
  we would have a group of 1 person who lives together within the infection     
  radius. These groups can move together like how individuals do in the current 
  implementation. These groups should be connected with some kind of lines to   
  show the connection between these people, and every group or every individual 
  should at most belong to one group, and that group should remain the same.    
  If you have marked 4 dots or 4 humans as a group, these are the 4 people who  
  stay together. We want to make sure that we don't have a case where we have 4 
  dots moving together, but then at a later point in time one of those starts   
  joining a different group. That's not how a typical society functions.        
  The other control that I would want to provide at the treatment and control   
  levels is the percentage of time any individual would spend outside of this   
  group. For example, every person across every group would have a certain      
  probability of leaving that group and meeting with other people, and they     
  would linger with other people for some time. This is to model families where 
  people stick together for 50% of the time, but the remaining time they would  
  go out for work and meet with other people and then spend some time within    
  the infection radius of other people. Once that time is over, they get back   
  to the same group where they started from.                                    
  Two additional knobs under the personal responsibility or personal control    
  section:                                                                      
  1. What is the probability that any individual can go outside of their family 
  group?                                                                        
  2. The linger time: how much time do they spend with other people?            
  Within those infection groups, I also want to make sure that the groups are   
  joined together through lines or some visual representation to really         
  understand that these people are a part of a group and that information is    
  made available visually.Again, ask me any clarifying questions.

⏺ User approved Claude's plan
  ⎿  Plan saved to: ~/.claude/plans/inherited-pondering-ripple.md · /plan to 
     edit
     Plan: Household ("population mode") for the SIR simulator

     Context

     The base tool (/Users/sakshamg/workspace/sims/) already simulates
     individuals wandering
     in a box with an SIR chart, A/B "arm" comparison, global controls, and a
     per-arm parameter
     schema. This change adds a more realistic population structure: instead of
     everyone
     moving independently, people live in households of 1–4 (tunable) people who
     stay
     together, and occasionally an individual leaves home to mix with others
     before returning.
     This lets the user study how household structure and "going out" behavior
     change the
     infection peak and total removed — the core question the tool exists to
     answer.

     Confirmed decisions (from clarification)

     - Population mode is a new global control with two options:
       - Individuals — current behavior (everyone independent).
       - Households — people partitioned into fixed groups of size 1..max.
     - Max household size — new global slider (default 4, range 1–8), only shown
     in
     household mode. Household sizes are drawn uniformly at random in
     1..maxHouseholdSize
     until the population is used up.
     - Households are permanent — membership is fixed for the whole run; a
     person belongs to
     exactly one household and never switches.
     - Households move as a unit (like an individual does today) and roam the
     box.
     - Two new per-arm knobs (under Personal control, shown only in household
     mode):
       a. Going-out probability (%) — chance per sim-second that a home person
     starts an outing.
       b. Time outside (sim-seconds) — how long each outing lasts. Temporal →
     scaled by the
     global time multiplier (like all temporal params, via the sim clock).
     - Out behavior — a person who leaves wanders freely across the box for the
     outing
     duration (mixing with anyone), then walks back to their household.
     - Social distancing — in household mode applies only to people while they 
     are out
     (households never distance internally). In individuals mode it is
     unchanged.
     - Visual — household members are connected by lines so groups are legible;
     a person
     who is out keeps a faint dashed tether to their home.
     - Determinism — household partition + member offsets are drawn from the
     seed in fixed
     order, so both arms get identical households under a shared seed; outing
     rolls use the
     per-step RNG (identical across arms when the outing params match).

     Design

     New/changed parameters (src/params.js)

     - Support a new optional flag requiresHouseholds: true on a param def
     (visible only in
     household mode).
     - Add global maxHouseholdSize (scope:'global', requiresHouseholds:true,
     min 1, max 8, step 1, default 4).
     - Add per-arm to Personal control (both requiresHouseholds:true):
       - goOutProbabilityPct — "Going-out probability", %, default 0.
       - timeOutside — "Time outside household", sim-sec, temporal:true,
     default 3.
     - populationMode is handled as app-level state (a <select>/segmented
     control), not a
     range slider; it is written into both arms' params.populationMode.

     UI plumbing (src/ui.js, index.html, src/app.js, styles.css)

     - App.UI.buildPanel(panelEl, params, onChange, opts) gains an
     opts.householdMode
     boolean; it skips requiresHouseholds params when false. applyDiff
     unchanged.
     - index.html: add a Population mode control (Individuals | Households) and
     keep the
     schema-driven #globalParams (which will now conditionally include
     maxHouseholdSize).
     - src/app.js:
       - buildGlobalParams() filters out requiresHouseholds global params when
     not in
     household mode (reuse the same pattern as ui.buildPanel).
       - Track populationMode (default 'individuals'), write it to both arms'
     params, and on
     change: rebuild global params + both arm panels (so the new knobs
     appear/disappear),
     then resizeCanvases() + resetAll() + updateDiff().
       - maxHouseholdSize change behaves like population (structural → both arms
     restart).
       - balanceArms() already copies all non-global per-arm params (the two new
     knobs
     included) — no change needed beyond a panel refresh().

     Engine (src/agent.js, src/simulation.js)

     - Agent: add householdId, homeDX/homeDY (fixed offset from anchor), and
     outing state
     outState ('home' | 'out' | 'returning') + outTimer.
     - Simulation.reset(): when params.populationMode === 'households', after
     creating agents,
     partition them into households of random size 1..maxHouseholdSize
     (deterministic),
     build this.households = [{anchorX, anchorY, vx, vy, members:[idx...]}],
     assign each member
     a fixed offset on a small circle of radius rc = min(10, 
     infectionRadius*0.4) around the
     anchor (so all members sit within one infection radius of each other), set
     outState='home'. Individuals mode leaves this.households = null and behaves
     as today.
     - Simulation._integrate():
       - Household mode movement: first advance each household anchor (existing
     wander +
     wall-bounce logic). Then per agent by outState:
           - home → position = anchor + offset (rigid cohesion); roll to leave
     with probability
     goOutProbabilityPct/100 * dt → if so, outState='out', outTimer=timeOutside,
     give a
     random heading.
         - out → free individual movement (reuse current wander + distancer
     steering, since
     distancing now applies to out people); outTimer -= dt; at <=0 →
     'returning'.
         - returning → steer toward the household anchor; when within rc →
     'home' (snap to
     offset).
       - Individuals mode: unchanged path (distancer steering as today).
       - Gate distancer steering so in household mode it only applies to
     out/returning agents.
       - Infection + recovery loop is unchanged — it runs on final positions, so
     intra-
     household spread and out-mixing both fall out naturally.
     - Simulation.render(): in household mode, before drawing dots, draw
     connecting lines
     from each household's anchor/centroid to each member (subtle light stroke);
     use a faint
     dashed line to out/returning members. Infection rings + dots unchanged.
     - Add refreshHouseholds()/reuse reset() for structural changes; outing
     params are read
     live each step (no rebuild).

     Critical files

     - src/params.js — new flag + params.
     - src/agent.js — new agent fields.
     - src/simulation.js — households build, movement state machine,
     connecting-line render.
     - src/ui.js — conditional param filtering in buildPanel.
     - src/app.js — population-mode control, conditional global params, panel
     rebuild on mode
     change.
     - index.html — population-mode control markup.
     - styles.css — styling for the mode control.

     Verification

     - Syntax: parse-only check of all src/*.js via osascript -l JavaScript
     (JavaScriptCore),
     as used previously (no Node available in this environment).
     - Headless engine test (JavaScriptCore, DOM-free): with
     populationMode='households',
     assert (a) every agent belongs to exactly one household and sizes are in
     1..max;
     (b) household partition is identical across two sims with the same seed;
     (c) with a
     higher goOutProbabilityPct, more people are seen out and inter-household
     spread /
     infection peak increases vs. 0%; (d) timeOutside scales the average outing
     duration.
     - Manual/browser: open index.html (still zero-dependency, offline).
     Confirm: switching
     to Households shows the max-size global slider and the two Personal-control
     knobs; dots
     cluster into connected groups that move together; raising going-out
     probability makes
     people periodically detach, wander, and return (tether visible); the SIR
     peak responds.
     (I cannot run a browser here — no Node for the browser tooling — so this
     step is for the user.)

     Out of scope

     - Real-world household-size distributions (using uniform random per the
     request).
     - Household-level social distancing (distancing applies only to out people,
     per decision).

       99 +  Simulation.prototype._buildHouseholds = function (rng) {    
      100 +    var agents = this.agents;                                 
      101 +    var n = agents.length;                                    
      102 +    var maxSize = Math.max(1, Math.min(8, Math.round(this.para
          +ms.maxHouseholdSize || 4)));                                  
      103 +    var rc = Math.min(10, this.params.infectionRadius * 0.4); 
          +// cluster radius                                             
      104 +    this.households = [];                                     
      105 +                                                              
      106 +    var idx = 0;                                              
      107 +    while (idx < n) {                                         
      108 +      var size = Math.min(rng.int(1, maxSize), n - idx);      
      109 +      var hid = this.households.length;                       
      110 +      var ax = agents[idx].x, ay = agents[idx].y; // anchor at
          + first member's spot                                          
      111 +      var ang0 = rng.range(0, Math.PI * 2);                   
      112 +      var h = {                                               
      113 +        anchorX: ax, anchorY: ay,                             
      114 +        vx: Math.cos(ang0), vy: Math.sin(ang0),               
      115 +        speedBase: rng.range(MIN_SPEED, MAX_SPEED),           
      116 +        members: []                                           
      117 +      };                                                      
      118 +      for (var m = 0; m < size; m++) {                        
      119 +        var ag = agents[idx];                                 
      120 +        ag.householdId = hid;                                 
      121 +        var off = size === 1 ? 0 : rc;                        
      122 +        var oa = (Math.PI * 2) * (m / size);                  
      123 +        ag.homeDX = Math.cos(oa) * off;                       
      124 +        ag.homeDY = Math.sin(oa) * off;                       
      125 +        ag.x = ax + ag.homeDX;                                
      126 +        ag.y = ay + ag.homeDY;                                
      127 +        ag.outState = 'home';                                 
      128 +        h.members.push(idx);                                  
      129 +        idx++;                                                
      130 +      }                                                       
      131 +      this.households.push(h);                                
      132 +    }                                                         
      133 +  };                                                          
      134 +

⏺ Good call — rigid offsets look lifeless. I'll make households breathe and 
  rotate: each member orbits the household anchor (the whole cluster slowly
  spins) and gently drifts closer/farther on a sine wave, while staying within
  the infection radius most of the time. It's driven off sim-time with
  per-member phases, so it's deterministic (identical across arms under a shared
  seed) and the connecting lines will animate along with it.

  direction).

  What I changed (all in src/agent.js + src/simulation.js):
  - Replaced the rigid member offset with an orbit: each member has an angular
  slot around the anchor, and the whole household rotates (rotSpeed varies per
  household and can spin either way).
  - Added breathing: each member's orbit distance oscillates on a sine wave with
  its own phase, so dots drift closer then farther.
  - The connecting lines animate along with this (they're drawn to each member's
  live position), so households now visibly swirl and pulse instead of sitting
  static.
  - Amplitude is tuned (HH_BREATHE_AMP = 0.28) so members stay within one
  infection radius of each other ~99% of the time. It's driven off sim-time with
  per-member phases, so it stays deterministic across arms.

  Note this freezes when the sim is paused (it's part of the simulation clock),
  which is consistent with the rest of the dynamics.

✻ Brewed for 2m 49s

❯ How does social distancing work in the household mode when the probability of 
  going outside the household is set to zero?                                   

⏺ Short answer: with going-out probability at 0%, the social-distancing control 
  does nothing in household mode.

  Here's why, from how it's implemented (src/simulation.js):

  - We decided earlier that in household mode, social distancing only applies to
  people while they're out. In the code, the distancing steer runs only inside
  the out branch — wander(a, /*allowDistance*/ true) is called only for agents
  in the out state. Agents that are home are slaved to their household's orbit
  position, and returning agents just steer straight back home — neither path
  ever applies distancing.
  - If going-out probability = 0, no one ever transitions from home → out, so no
  agent is ever in the state where distancing is active. The
  socialDistancingPct slider still flags some people as distancers
  (isDistancer), but that flag is never consulted, so it has zero effect on the
  simulation.

  So in that specific configuration:
  - Households still roam the box as units and can collide with other
  households, and infection still spreads between them on contact.
  - Social distancing can't prevent any of that, because home members don't
  distance and a household moves as one body.

  That's a real limitation of the current model — at 0% going-out, distancing is
  inert. If you'd like it to matter in that scenario, the natural fix is
  household-level distancing: a "distancer household" would steer its whole 
  anchor away from other households (families keep their distance from other
  families), while members still cluster with their own family. That was
  explicitly out of scope before, but it's a clean addition.

  Want me to add household-level distancing so the control has an effect even
  when nobody goes out? Options:
  - A) Keep as-is (distancing only affects people who are out).
  - B) Add household-level distancing (whole households avoid each other) in 
  addition to out-person distancing.

✻ Brewed for 33s

❯ I think you need to change that model. When social distancing is set in the   
  household model, but there is zero probability of anyone else going outside   
  that household, then the limit or the social distancing rules should apply at 
  the household level. No household should come closer to any other             
  household's infection radius if the social distancing is set to 100%. If it's 
  set to 50%, then 50% of the households can come within the infection radius   
  of another household. When the going-out probability is set to a nonzero      
  number, then the social distancing rules apply to both the household and the  
  individual who's going out. The individual who's going out will maintain a    
  certain probability of getting within the infection radius of some other      
  household or some other individual. Similarly, a certain percentage of those  
  households would adhere to the same rules as well: they shouldn't come near   
  the infection radius of another individual or another household.              
                                                                                
  One clarification is that when we say households respecting social            
  distancing, this would mean that if any member of the household comes within  
  the infection radius of any other person in any other household, that would   
  mean that a household is essentially within the infection radius of another   
  household.  Ask me any clarifying questions or point out in case there are    
  issues with this plan.                                                        

⏺ This is a meaningful model change and you explicitly asked me to flag issues,
  so let me plan it carefully. I know this code well from building it, so I'll
  ground the design and surface the real trade-offs rather than spawn explorers.

  Let me confirm my understanding of the new model:
  - socialDistancingPct in household mode = % of households that are "distancer
  households."
  - A distancer household keeps its members from coming within the infection
  radius of anyone in another household (you defined "household A within
  household B's radius" = any member of A within infection radius of any member
  of B).
  - At 0% going-out: distancing acts purely at the household level (distancer
  households keep their whole cluster away from other households).
  - At >0% going-out: distancing applies at both levels — the distancer
  household avoids others, and its members, when out, distance from other
  people/households too.

  Two genuine design points I want to settle before writing the plan:

⏺ User answered Claude's questions:
  ⎿  · How strictly should household distancing be enforced? A hard guarantee 
     has a real physical problem: in a crowded box (high population + high 
     distancing %) there may be no room for every household to stay outside 
     everyone else's radius, so a hard rule can jam/freeze households or be 
     impossible to satisfy. → Soft steering (recommended)
     · How should a person's distancing status be assigned in household mode? → 
     Per household, inherited (recommended)

⏺ Both confirmed: soft steering and per-household inherited status. I have
  everything I need. Let me write the plan.

⏺ Updated plan
  ⎿  /plan to preview

⏺ The plan is written. Let me present it for approval.

⏺ User approved Claude's plan
  ⎿  Plan saved to: ~/.claude/plans/inherited-pondering-ripple.md · /plan to 
     edit
     Plan: Household-level social distancing

     Context

     In the current build, social distancing in household mode only affects
     people while
     they are out of their household. So when going-out probability = 0, nobody
     is ever
     out and the social-distancing slider does nothing — even though households
     still roam the
     box and collide, spreading infection with no way to prevent it. This change
     makes social
     distancing meaningful at the household level, matching how real societies
     distance:
     whole families keep away from other families.

     New model (confirmed)

     - In household mode, socialDistancingPct = the percentage of households
     that are
     "distancer households." One roll per household; all members inherit it.
     - A distancer household keeps its members out of the infection radius of
     anyone in
     another household. (Definition: household A is "within" household B's
     radius if any
     member of A is within the infection radius of any member of B —
     approximated below by
     keeping household anchors far enough apart that members can't overlap.)
     - Going-out probability = 0 → distancing acts purely at the household 
     level:
     distancer households steer their whole cluster away from other households.
     At 100% no
     household enters another's radius (so inter-household spread ≈ 0); at 50%
     only the ~50%
     non-distancer households can still contact others.
     - Going-out probability > 0 → distancing applies at both levels: the
     household
     avoids other households, and its members distance as individuals while out
     (this already
     works via the per-agent isDistancer flag, which now comes from the
     household).
     - Enforcement = soft steering (a strong avoidance force, like the existing
     individual
     distancing), not a hard constraint — see caveats.
     - Individuals mode is unchanged (per-individual distancing as today).

     Design (all in src/simulation.js, plus one help-text tweak)

     Per-household distancer flag

     - _buildHouseholds(rng): give each household h._distanceRoll = rng.next()
     (drawn in
     fixed order → identical across arms under a shared seed).
     - _applyBehaviorFlags() (runs on reset and on live socialDistancingPct
     edits via
     refreshBehavior()):
       - Individuals mode: unchanged (a.isDistancer = a._distanceRoll < dPct).
       - Households mode: h.isDistancer = h._distanceRoll < dPct per household,
     then each
     member a.isDistancer = h.isDistancer. willQuarantine stays per-agent.
     This means changing the slider live re-flags households with no rebuild.

     Household-level avoidance (soft steering)

     - In _integrate, the household-anchor movement loop: for each distancer
     household,
     accumulate a repulsion vector away from every other household anchor within
     avoidDist, and blend it into the anchor's heading before integrating (same
     pattern as
     the individual distancer steer: add rep * DISTANCE_STEER * dt, then
     renormalize).
       - rc = min(10, infectionRadius*0.4) (already computed in the household
     branch).
       - avoidDist = infectionRadius + 2*rc*(1 + HH_BREATHE_AMP) + 2 — the
     anchor separation
     below which two households' orbiting/breathing members could enter each
     other's radius.
       - Non-distancer households: wander only (unchanged). Repulsion adds no
     RNG draws, so
     cross-arm determinism is preserved.
     - Out-individual distancing needs no change: the out branch already calls
     wander(a, /*allowDistance*/ true), and a.isDistancer is now the inherited
     household
     flag, so members of distancer households distance while out.

     Help text (src/params.js)

     - Update the socialDistancingPct help to explain the dual meaning: in
     Households mode it
     is the % of households that keep their family away from other households
     (and whose members
     also distance when out).

     Known issues / caveats (surfaced per request)

     1. Soft, not absolute. Steering strongly avoids contact but does not
     guarantee zero
     incursions — consistent with the existing individual distancing. Occasional
     brief
     overlaps can happen, especially while breathing/rotating.
     2. Crowding can make full separation physically impossible. At high
     population + high
     distancing %, there isn't enough room for every household to stay outside
     everyone's
     radius; soft steering degrades gracefully (households pack as far apart as
     they can)
     rather than jamming, which a hard constraint would risk.
     3. Anchor-based approximation. Household avoidance keys on anchor-to-anchor
     distance
     with padding (fast, and equivalent in effect to the member-to-member
     definition). A
     distancer household does not specifically dodge a lone out-person from a
     non-distancer
     household — that out-person only keeps clear if they themselves are a
     distancer. This is
     a minor edge case at nonzero going-out.

     Critical files

     - src/simulation.js — _buildHouseholds (household roll),
     _applyBehaviorFlags
     (per-household flag + inheritance), _integrate (anchor repulsion for
     distancer
     households).
     - src/params.js — socialDistancingPct help text only.

     Verification

     - Syntax: parse-only check of src/*.js via osascript -l JavaScript (no Node
     here).
     - Headless engine test (JavaScriptCore, DOM-free), household mode,
     goOutProbabilityPct=0:
       - 0% distancing → high inter-household spread (large total removed).
       - 100% distancing → spread contained to the initially-infected households
     (total
     removed ≈ just those households' members) — assert removed(100%) ≪
     removed(0%).
       - 50% → intermediate.
       - Assert members of distancer households have isDistancer=true and
     inherit correctly;
     confirm same-seed determinism holds.
     - Manual/browser: open index.html (still zero-dependency, offline).
     Households mode,
     going-out 0%, social distancing 100%: distancer households visibly keep
     apart and the
     epidemic barely spreads beyond the initial homes; at 50% about half still
     mingle.
     (I cannot drive a browser here — no Node — so this step is for the user.)

     Out of scope

     - Hard collision-based separation (using soft steering per decision).
     - Distancer households dodging lone out-individuals from non-distancer
     households (caveat 3).

⏺ The new model works exactly as intended (going-out = 0%):

  ┌──────────────────┬─────────────────────┬──────────────┬───────────────┐
  │     Social       │     Distancer       │    Peak      │ Total removed │
  │    distancing    │     households      │   infected   │               │
  ├──────────────────┼─────────────────────┼──────────────┼───────────────┤
  │ 0%               │ 0 / 89              │ 160          │ 200           │
  │                  │                     │              │ (everyone)    │
  ├──────────────────┼─────────────────────┼──────────────┼───────────────┤
  │ 50%              │ 54 / 89             │ 128          │ 193           │
  ├──────────────────┼─────────────────────┼──────────────┼───────────────┤
  │ 100%             │ 89 / 89             │ 10           │ 17            │
  └──────────────────┴─────────────────────┴──────────────┴───────────────┘

  - 100% → spread is contained to essentially just the initially-infected
  households (~17 people from the 3 seed infections' families) — no household
  enters another's radius, so it barely escapes the initial homes.
  - 50% → the peak flattens (160→128); total removed drops only a little because
  the ~35 non-distancer households still roam and carry it around — which is
  the realistic behavior you described ("50% can still come within range").
  - Inheritance is correct (members of distancer households are flagged) and
  same-seed determinism holds.

  What I changed (src/simulation.js + one help string in src/params.js):
  - Per-household distancer roll in _buildHouseholds.
  - _applyBehaviorFlags now decides distancing per household in household mode
  and inherits it to members (updates live when you move the slider, no
  rebuild). Individuals mode unchanged.
  - Distancer households steer their anchor away from other households (soft
  repulsion, keyed on avoidDist = infectionRadius + 2·rc·(1+breathe) + 2), so it
  works even at 0% going-out. Out-members already distance individually via the
  inherited flag.

  Caveats from the plan still hold: it's soft steering (strong avoidance, not an
  absolute guarantee), and at very high population + high distancing there may
  not be room to separate everyone.

  household distancing), or one combined — your call.

⏺ Makes sense — a hard guarantee when there's room, gracefully degrading to
  best-effort when the box saturates. I'll keep the soft steer (for smooth,
  natural avoidance velocities) and add a positional separation pass after the
  anchors move: it pushes distancer households apart until they're at least
  avoidDist from every other household, run for a few relaxation iterations so
  it resolves multi-way crowding and simply does its best when space runs out.

⏺ Household distancing is now a hard constraint with graceful fallback. Summary:

  What changed (src/simulation.js):
  - Kept the soft steering (so distancer households move away smoothly), then
  added a positional separation pass after anchors move: for any pair where at
  least one household distances, it pushes them apart to avoidDist, running 4
  relaxation iterations to resolve multi-way crowding. Only the distancer(s)
  yield (both distancers split the push 50/50), and anchors stay in-bounds. No
  RNG used, so cross-arm determinism holds.

  Verified (household mode, going-out = 0%):

  ┌──────────────────────────┬──────────────────────┬──────────────────────┐
  │           Case           │   Cross-household    │    Total removed     │
  │                          │      incursions      │                      │
  ├──────────────────────────┼──────────────────────┼──────────────────────┤
  │ pop 200, distancing 0%   │ 245                  │ 200                  │
  ├──────────────────────────┼──────────────────────┼──────────────────────┤
  │ pop 200, distancing 100% │ 4 (transient, at     │ 3 — contained to     │
  │                          │ walls)               │ initial cases        │
  ├──────────────────────────┼──────────────────────┼──────────────────────┤
  │ pop 500, distancing 100% │ 891 (best-effort)    │ 4 — still contained  │
  │  (saturated)             │                      │                      │
  └──────────────────────────┴──────────────────────┴──────────────────────┘

  - At 100% in a non-saturated box, distancer households essentially never let
  their members into another household's radius — the 4 residual touches are
  momentary wall/corner squeezes that transmit nothing (removed stays at the 3
  seed infections).
  - Under saturation (pop 500), it degrades to best-effort without jamming or
  crashing, and still keeps transmission near zero.

  So distancing now "completely avoids" other households when there's room, and
  does best-effort when packed — as requested.

⏺ I dug into this and want to give you a precise answer. With the default 
  settings I can't reproduce "the infection gets contained at 10%" — at 10%
  distancing the epidemic still spreads to 196–200 / 200 for both arm seeds, and
  the initially‑infected household (H0) is not a distancer. So the core model
  is behaving. Here's what's actually going on, and the one real quirk that can
  make it look like what you're describing.

  1. What distancing does (by design): distancing is decided per household — at
  10%, ~10% of households (this seed: 15/89) are "distancer households." A
  distancer household steers away from every other household, not just infected
  ones (realistically, you keep your distance from everyone because you don't
  know who's infected). So when the roaming infected household passes near one
  of those ~15 distancer households, that household darts away.

  2. What you're probably seeing on screen: you're watching the red infected
  household, and the handful of distancer households near it scatter away — so
  it looks like "the infected household is repelling/avoiding everyone." But
  it's those specific distancer households avoiding all contact. The other ~90%
  of households don't flee and still get infected — which is why the SIR curve
  still climbs to ~196.

  3. The real quirk worth fixing: all initial infections are seeded into the
  first household(s) (agents 0,1,2). If that one household happens to draw the
  distancer flag — a ~10–27% coin flip depending on seed — the entire outbreak
  starts isolated and barely escapes, even at 10%. That produces exactly the
  "even at 10% everything is contained" surprise, and it's pure luck of whether
  the seed household is a distancer. That's a genuine modeling artifact: the
  outcome shouldn't hinge on one household.

  Recommended fix: seed the initial infections across random, distinct 
  households (deterministically via the RNG) instead of always the first
  household. Then the outbreak starts from several points and can't be locked
  down by one household's distancer flag, and low-% distancing behaves
  intuitively.

  Want me to make that change? I can also add a subtle visual marker for
  distancer households so it's clear which ones are choosing to keep away.

✻ Cogitated for 4m 43s

※ recap: We're building a browser-based SIR epidemic simulator with A/B arms; currently in household mode, initial infections all seed into the first household, so low distancing can accidentally contain the outbreak. Next: seed initial infections across random households, pending your go-ahead.

❯ during social distncing, when are individuals and households marked as the    
  ones who would distance?                                                      

⏺ Here's exactly when and how the "who distances" decision is made — it happens
  in two stages: a fixed random roll assigned at reset, and a threshold 
  comparison that turns the slider percentage into flags.

  Stage 1 — the roll (assigned once, at reset/rebuild):
  - Individuals: in Simulation.reset() each agent gets a._distanceRoll = 
  rng.next() (a fixed number in [0,1)) as it's created (src/simulation.js).
  - Households: in _buildHouseholds() each household gets h._distanceRoll = 
  rng.next().

  These rolls are drawn from the seeded RNG in a fixed order, so they're stable 
  for the whole run and identical across both arms under a shared seed. They are
  not re-rolled when you move the slider.

  Stage 2 — the flag (computed in _applyBehaviorFlags()):
  With dPct = socialDistancingPct / 100:
  - Individuals mode: a.isDistancer = a._distanceRoll < dPct.
  - Households mode: h.isDistancer = h._distanceRoll < dPct per household, then
  every member inherits it: member.isDistancer = h.isDistancer.

  When _applyBehaviorFlags() runs:
  1. On every reset (new population/households built), and
  2. On every live change of the Social distancing slider — onParamChange in
  src/app.js calls sim.refreshBehavior(), which re-runs _applyBehaviorFlags()
  without rebuilding the population.

  Why the roll < pct design matters: because the roll is fixed and only the
  threshold moves, raising the slider only adds distancers to the existing set
  (nested/monotonic) — the same people/households stay distancers as you go from
  10%→20%, and both arms pick the identical set for a fair comparison. That's
  also why "10%" for a given seed can land at 15/89 households: it's however
  many happened to roll below 0.10 (averages ~10% across seeds).

  Where the flag is then used (in _integrate):
  - Individuals mode: each agent's isDistancer drives its avoidance steering
  every step.
  - Households mode: h.isDistancer drives the household-level anchor separation,
  and a member's inherited isDistancer makes them distance individually while 
  they're out.

  So the assignment is: rolled once at reset (per-agent or per-household), then
  flagged live by comparing that roll to the current percentage — with household
  members inheriting their household's decision.

  ⎿  Plan saved to: ~/.claude/plans/inherited-pondering-ripple.md · /plan to 
     edit
     Plan: Diurnal going-out cycle + cleaner household connecting lines

     Context

     Two household-mode refinements:

     1. Connecting lines form a hub "vertex." Today the lines radiate from each
     household's invisible anchor point, so spokes appear to meet at empty
     space. Switch
     to a star centered on a real member (a hub dot), and draw no line to 
     members who
     are out. Every line then runs dot→dot and looks intentional.
     2. Going out is currently random/desynchronized (a per-step Poisson leave
     with a
     personal outTimer). Real societies leave together in the morning and return
     together in the evening. Replace the Poisson mechanic with a diurnal 
     schedule:
     a global Day length; each day ~`goOutProbability%of people depart around a 
     global morning time and return around a global evening time (= morning 
     +time outside`), each
     with up to ±1 hour of variance.

     Confirmed decisions

     - Lines: star from one real (home) member per household; skip out/returning
     members.
     - Day cycle: add global Day length; goOutProbability = chance a person goes
     out
     on a given day; time outside = length of the workday out; departures
     cluster near a
     global morning time, returns near global evening (morning + time outside);
     both jittered
     by up to ±1 hour (1 hour = dayLength/24). Day length is temporal (scales
     with the time
     multiplier), and global (environmental) + requiresHouseholds.

     Design

     Schema (src/params.js)

     - Add global dayLength — group "Simulation & environment", scope:'global',
     requiresHouseholds:true, temporal:true, min 5, max 60, step 1, default 15,
     unit s.
     Appears automatically via buildGlobalParams (already filters
     requiresHouseholds).
     - Update help text: goOutProbabilityPct → "chance a person goes out on a
     given day";
     timeOutside → "length of the workday spent out; people return around
     morning + this."

     Agent (src/agent.js)

     - Replace the now-unused outTimer role with a per-day schedule:
     _schedDay = -1, goToday = false, departTime = 0, returnTime = 0.

     Engine (src/simulation.js, _integrate household branch)

     - Constants: MORNING_PHASE = 0.30 (fraction of the day when the workday
     starts).
     - Per agent, lazily (re)compute the day's schedule when the day index
     changes:
       - day = Math.floor(this.time / dayLength); if a._schedDay !== day,
     recompute using a
     dedicated deterministic RNG seeded from (seed, day, agentIndex)
     (independent of
     frame timing, identical across arms when params match):
           - HOUR = dayLength / 24; dayStart = day * dayLength;
     morning = dayStart + MORNING_PHASE * dayLength.
         - a.goToday = r.next() < goOutProbabilityPct/100.
         - a.departTime = morning + (r.next()*2-1)*HOUR.
         - a.returnTime = morning + timeOutside + (r.next()*2-1)*HOUR, clamped
     so
     departTime + HOUR ≤ returnTime ≤ dayStart + 0.97*dayLength (stay within the
     day).
         - a._schedDay = day.
       - Transitions (replacing the Poisson leave + outTimer):
           - home → if goToday && time ≥ departTime && time < returnTime → out
     (give random
     heading). Otherwise stay clamped to homeTarget.
         - out → wander (distancing applies to out people, unchanged); if time ≥
     returnTime
     → returning.
         - returning → steer to homeTarget; snap to home when within rc
     (unchanged).
     - Net effect: at a global morning the going-out cohort departs together (±1
     hr), works for
     timeOutside, and returns together in the evening (±1 hr).
     goOutProbabilityPct = 0 →
     nobody leaves (pure household mode), as today.

     Render (src/simulation.js, Pass 0)

     - For each household: pick hub = first member with outState === 'home'
     (skip household
     if none home). Draw a solid straight line from hub to each other home
     member.
     Do not draw lines to out/returning members. Single stroke style, no dashes.

     Critical files

     - src/params.js — dayLength global param + help-text updates.
     - src/agent.js — per-day schedule fields.
     - src/simulation.js — schedule-driven outing in _integrate; star-from-hub
     lines in
     render.
     - src/app.js — no change expected (dayLength flows through the
     schema-driven global
     controls; global-param changes already reset both arms).

     Verification

     - Syntax: parse-only check of src/*.js via osascript -l JavaScript.
     - Headless engine test (JavaScriptCore), household mode,
     goOutProbabilityPct=60,
     dayLength=15, timeOutside=3:
       - Sample the count of out agents over one day → it should be ~0 at night,
     spike near
     the morning phase, and drop back near evening (clustered, not uniform) —
     contrasted
     with a flat distribution under the old model.
       - Over a day, the fraction of people who went out ≈ goOutProbabilityPct.
       - Departure times fall within ±1 hour of morning; return times within ±1
     hour of
     morning+timeOutside.
       - Same-seed determinism holds across two sims.
     - Manual/browser: open index.html, Households mode, going-out > 0: dots
     stay home,
     then most leave around the same time, roam, and stream back together later;
     household
     lines are a clean star on a real dot with no lines to those who are out.
     (No browser here — no Node — so this step is for the user.)

     Out of scope

     - Per-arm day length (kept global/environmental).
     - Multiple daily trips (one outing per person per day).

  ┌─────────────────────┬────────────────────┐

  ├─────────────────────┼────────────────────┤
  │ 0.0–0.2 (night)     │ ~0                 │
  ├─────────────────────┼────────────────────┤
  │ 0.3 (morning)       │ 108 ← departure    │
  ├─────────────────────┼────────────────────┤
  │ 0.4 (midday)        │ 120 (peak)         │
  ├─────────────────────┼────────────────────┤
  │ 0.5                 │ 80                 │
  ├─────────────────────┼────────────────────┤
  │ 0.6 → 0.7 (evening) │ 19 → 2 ← returning │
  ├─────────────────────┼────────────────────┤
  │ 0.8–0.9 (night)     │ 0                  │
  └─────────────────────┴────────────────────┘

  global morning (±~1 hr) and return around evening (morning + time outside, ±~1

※ recap: You're building a standalone browser SIR epidemic simulator with A/B arms; I just finished the diurnal going-out cycle and cleaner household connecting lines, all verified. Next action is committing and pushing those changes when you're ready.

❯ do we need time outside household now?                                        

⏺ Yes — it's still doing real work, just in a new role. With the day cycle, Time
  outside household now sets the length of the workday — a person's evening
  return time is morning + timeOutside (±~1 hr). So it directly controls:

  - When people come home in the evening (bigger value → they stay out later).
  - How long each person is exposed to others while out, which materially
  affects transmission — a quick errand (small value) spreads far less than a
  full day out mingling (large value).

  It's not redundant with the other two knobs:
  - Day length = the full cycle length (including night at home).
  - Going-out probability = whether someone goes out on a given day.
  - Time outside = how long they're out once they do.

  (It's clamped to stay within the day, so it effectively ranges from a short
  trip up to ~most of the day.)

  So I'd keep it. That said, if you'd rather simplify — e.g., make "everyone out
  for the whole daytime window" and drop the knob — I can replace it with a
  fixed daytime window instead. Want to keep it, or simplify it away?
